Or I’m holding the stop-slow paddle, and I know this is my job, I know I know how to do it, but the steps just vanish. My mind goes blank right there, usually at the worst possible time, when I need to be quick and clear.\n\nAnd then of course I get anxious because I’m blanking, so it sort of feeds itself. I start thinking, “Did I already do that part? What comes next? Am I missing something important?” But the blankness feels like it comes first sometimes, and then the stress piles on after.\n\nDoctor: During these blank moments, do you ever find yourself doing repetitive movements you didn’t mean to, like lip-smacking, chewing, or fumbling with your hands?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. I mean, I’m usually so focused on the fact that my mind has gone blank that I’m trying really hard to look normal and not make a mistake, so I might miss little things. But I haven’t had anyone say, like, “Why are you chewing like that?” or “Why are you moving your hands funny?” or anything.\n\nI do sometimes fidget when I’m anxious, like holding the paperwork tighter or adjusting my grip on the paddle, but that feels more like me being tense and trying to get myself back on track. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by repetitive movements. I haven’t noticed lip-smacking or chewing or anything that feels automatic like that.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank moments passes, can you remember what happened during it?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… but it’s patchy in a weird way. I can usually remember that I was standing there, or sitting with the road-closure forms in front of me, and I remember staring at them or holding the stop-slow paddle and thinking, “Why can’t I do this?” So it’s not like the whole thing disappears afterward.\n\nWhat I don’t always remember clearly is what I was thinking right in the middle, because there almost isn’t anything there to remember. It’s like my mind just goes blank at the worst possible moment. I’ll be looking at something I’ve done for years, something routine, and suddenly I can’t bring up the next step. Then once it passes, I can remember being stuck, and I can remember the panic of trying to figure out, “Did I already do that? What comes next?” But the actual blank part is just… empty.\n\nSo I guess I remember the situation around it, but not in a useful way. I don’t come out of it with a clear memory of the steps or what I did during it. That’s part of why it scares me, because these are things I should know automatically by now.\n\nDoctor: About how long does a typical blank moment last?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to pin down exactly, because when it happens I’m usually trying so hard to get myself back, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m calmly timing it. I know I’ve done them for years, I know they should be familiar, but for that stretch I just can’t pull the steps into my head. Or with the stop-slow paddle, it might only be a short time before I can sort of force myself back into the routine, but standing there with traffic and people expecting me to know what comes next, it feels endless.\n\nSo… typical? I’d say usually brief, maybe under a couple minutes, but it’s long enough to scare me because it’s at the worst possible times.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank moments ends, do you feel confused?\n\nPatient: Yes… kind of. Like I’ll come out of it and I’m still looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and I know I should know the next step because I’ve done it for years, but it’s like the order of it gets scrambled or just disappears for a minute.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst times. I’ll be in the middle of something routine, something I shouldn’t even have to think that hard about, and suddenly my mind just goes blank. Then afterward I’m left trying to piece it back together—like, “Okay, did I already do this part? What comes next? Am I missing something obvious?” And that makes me feel confused and embarrassed, because it’s work I know I know, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, after it ends I’m usually confused for a bit, mostly trying to get the steps back in the right order and act like nothing happened.\n\nDoctor: During one of these blank moments, if someone talks to you, can you respond right away?\n\nPatient: Not always right away, no. It’s more like there’s this gap where my thinking just stopped working, and then I’m left trying to catch up and figure out what I missed.\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me enough to come in. It’s happening at the worst times, when I’m supposed to be focused and I should absolutely know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found that time had passed or that you had done something during one of these spells that you couldn’t remember afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve found that I did some whole thing and then had no memory of it, like… I can’t point to a completed task and say, “I don’t remember doing that at all.” It’s more that I’ll be in the middle of something very familiar and my mind just goes blank.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It’s not like a whole chunk of time is missing exactly, at least not that I can always prove, but after it happens I’ll realize I don’t really know what I just did for a few seconds, or what someone said to me right then.\n\nThe bigger thing is that my mind just goes blank in the middle of something I should know. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps. And these aren’t new tasks—I’ve done them for years. So afterward I’m trying to piece it together, like, “Did I already check that part? Did someone ask me something? Was I supposed to move on to the next step?” It’s hard to explain because it’s not always a clear memory loss, like waking up and not knowing where I am. It’s more like there’s this gap where my thinking just stopped working, and then I’m left trying to catch up and figure out what I missed.\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me enough to come in. It’s happening at the worst times, when I’m supposed to be focused and I should absolutely know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found that time had passed or that you had done something during one of these spells that you couldn’t remember afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve found that I did some whole thing and then had no memory of it, like… I can’t point to a completed task and say, “I don’t remember doing that at all.” It’s more that I’ll be in the middle of something very familiar and my mind just goes blank.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember what comes next. And that’s not something that should be happening, because I’ve done those steps for years. It’s not complicated to me normally. But in that moment it’s like the order of it disappears, and I’m standing there trying to pull it back.\n\nSo afterward I might feel like time slipped a little because I’m trying to figure out, “Wait, did I already do that part? Was I supposed to be on the next step?” But I’m not sure it’s the same as doing things and not remembering them. The part I know for sure is that my thinking just cuts out at the worst times, with things I should absolutely know how to do.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever lost control of your bladder during one of these blank spells?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 41 / crossing_guard_or_flagger / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t noticed anything like that happening. After these spells I’m usually embarrassed or scared because I’ve lost my place mentally, but I haven’t found that I wet myself or anything like that.

I guess if it had happened at work I’d probably know, because I’m standing outside or around other people, and that would be pretty obvious. So… no, not that I’m aware of.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nG40.909, F44.9, R41.3, F06.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I’m having these moments where my mind just goes blank, and it’s starting to scare me. Like, at work, I’ve done the same road-closure paperwork and the same stop-slow paddle routine for years, I know the steps, I really do—but lately I’ll be looking right at the forms or holding the paddle, and it’s like the instructions just disappear out of my head.\n\nIt happens at the worst times, too, not just when I’m sitting around. I’ll be standing there thinking, okay, I should know what comes next, and there’s just nothing. Blank. And then I get stuck trying to remember what I already did and what I’m supposed to do next. That’s why I’m here. It feels like my own mind isn’t reliable right when I need it to be.\n\nDoctor: When these blank moments happen, do you lose awareness of what’s going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I fully black out or anything, if that’s what you mean. I’m still there, like I can see the cones, the cars, the paperwork in front of me, or I’m holding the stop-slow paddle and I know I’m supposed to be doing the job. But the part that tells me what comes next just… drops out.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I’m aware enough to realize something is wrong, which almost makes it worse. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms I’ve filled out so many times, or standing there with the paddle, and suddenly I cannot pull up the steps I’ve done for years. It’s not like I don’t know where I am. It’s more like my thinking goes empty right when I need it, and then I’m stuck trying to piece together what I already did and what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank moments tend to happen when you’re feeling especially stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think stress makes it worse, or at least it’s when I notice it the most, because there’s pressure to get it right. But it’s not only like, “Oh, I’m nervous so I forgot something.” It feels bigger than that.\n\nLike I’ll be at work, and I’ll have the road-closure forms in front of me—forms I’ve done over and over, for years—and I’ll just stare at them and suddenly I can’t remember the order. Or I’m holding the stop-slow paddle, and I know this is my job, I know I know how to do it, but the steps just vanish. My mind goes blank right there, usually at the worst possible time, when I need to be quick and clear.\n\nAnd then of course I get anxious because I’m blanking, so it sort of feeds itself. I start thinking, “Did I already do that part? What comes next? Am I missing something important?” But the blankness feels like it comes first sometimes, and then the stress piles on after.\n\nDoctor: During these blank moments, do you ever find yourself doing repetitive movements you didn’t mean to, like lip-smacking, chewing, or fumbling with your hands?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. I mean, I’m usually so focused on the fact that my mind has gone blank that I’m trying really hard to look normal and not make a mistake, so I might miss little things. But I haven’t had anyone say, like, “Why are you chewing like that?” or “Why are you moving your hands funny?” or anything.\n\nI do sometimes fidget when I’m anxious, like holding the paperwork tighter or adjusting my grip on the paddle, but that feels more like me being tense and trying to get myself back on track. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by repetitive movements. I haven’t noticed lip-smacking or chewing or anything that feels automatic like that.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank moments passes, can you remember what happened during it?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… but it’s patchy in a weird way. I can usually remember that I was standing there, or sitting with the road-closure forms in front of me, and I remember staring at them or holding the stop-slow paddle and thinking, “Why can’t I do this?” So it’s not like the whole thing disappears afterward.\n\nWhat I don’t always remember clearly is what I was thinking right in the middle, because there almost isn’t anything there to remember. It’s like my mind just goes blank at the worst possible moment. I’ll be looking at something I’ve done for years, something routine, and suddenly I can’t bring up the next step. Then once it passes, I can remember being stuck, and I can remember the panic of trying to figure out, “Did I already do that? What comes next?” But the actual blank part is just… empty.\n\nSo I guess I remember the situation around it, but not in a useful way. I don’t come out of it with a clear memory of the steps or what I did during it. That’s part of why it scares me, because these are things I should know automatically by now.\n\nDoctor: About how long does a typical blank moment last?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to pin down exactly, because when it happens I’m usually trying so hard to get myself back, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m calmly timing it. But most of the time it feels like maybe seconds to a minute or two… though honestly it can feel much longer in the moment, because it happens right when I need to know what I’m doing.\n\nLike with the road-closure forms, I’ll just be looking at them and suddenly the order is gone. I know I’ve done them for years, I know they should be familiar, but for that stretch I just can’t pull the steps into my head. Or with the stop-slow paddle, it might only be a short time before I can sort of force myself back into the routine, but standing there with traffic and people expecting me to know what comes next, it feels endless.\n\nSo… typical? I’d say usually brief, maybe under a couple minutes, but it’s long enough to scare me because it’s at the worst possible times.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank moments ends, do you feel confused?\n\nPatient: Yes… kind of. Not confused like I don’t know where I am or who I’m with, but confused about what I was supposed to be doing. Like I’ll come out of it and I’m still looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and I know I should know the next step because I’ve done it for years, but it’s like the order of it gets scrambled or just disappears for a minute.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst times. I’ll be in the middle of something routine, something I shouldn’t even have to think that hard about, and suddenly my mind just goes blank. Then afterward I’m left trying to piece it back together—like, “Okay, did I already do this part? What comes next? Am I missing something obvious?” And that makes me feel confused and embarrassed, because it’s work I know I know, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, after it ends I’m usually confused for a bit, mostly trying to get the steps back in the right order and act like nothing happened.\n\nDoctor: During one of these blank moments, if someone talks to you, can you respond right away?\n\nPatient: Not always right away, no. I mean, I can sometimes hear that someone has said something, like I register there’s a voice or that they’re waiting for me, but the answer doesn’t come immediately because my mind is still stuck on… nothing, almost.\n\nLike if I’m staring at the road-closure forms and someone asks, “Did you finish that part?” or “What’s next?” I might just look at them for a second too long because I suddenly can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s embarrassing, because I know I should be able to answer quickly. Same with the stop-slow paddle—if I’m holding it and someone talks to me, I might respond, but it can be delayed or kind of uncertain, because I’m trying to pull my thinking back together.\n\nSo I don’t think I’m completely unaware, exactly. It’s more like there’s a lag. I hear them, but my brain won’t give me the next step or the right words fast enough.\n\nDoctor: Right before one of these blank moments starts, do you ever notice a strange smell or taste?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed anything like a weird smell or taste right before it happens.\n\nUsually it’s more like I’m just doing the task and then suddenly I realize my mind has gone blank. I’m not aware of a warning sign like that. If there is one, I’m not catching it, because I’m usually focused on the forms or the road or what I’m supposed to be doing, and then it just kind of… drops out.\n\nSo, no, not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever told you that during one of these blank moments you just stare and don’t respond for a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m passed out or anything, but people have noticed that I’ll sort of freeze or stare for a few seconds and not answer like I normally would.\n\nIt usually happens when I’m right in the middle of something I should know how to do. That’s honestly why I came in—my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll be holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps. Steps I’ve done for years. And if someone says something to me right then, I guess from the outside it probably looks like I’m just staring and not responding.\n\nSometimes someone will be waiting on me, and I can tell afterward that there was this pause where I didn’t answer. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to ignore them. I’m just stuck for that moment, like my brain won’t move to the next part. Then I come back into it and try to cover it, but I know they’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Between these blank moments, do you spend most days feeling hard-to-control worry about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I do think about things, and I get concerned about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “most days” or “a lot of different things” in the way you mean.\n\nLike, when you say hard-to-control worry, I’m trying to figure out if you mean worry that just runs on its own and I can’t shut it off, or if you mean being stressed about what’s happening. Because I can get caught up thinking about something, especially if something went wrong or I’m afraid I missed something, but I don’t know that I’ve really been keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it as a clear pattern like that, at least not enough to say yes confidently.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that during one of these blank spells you seemed completely unaware of them or of what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely unaware. That’s the part I have trouble judging, because from inside it doesn’t feel like I’m gone somewhere exactly. It’s more like my thinking just blanks out.\n\nLike, that’s really why I came in. I’ll be right there at work, looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and then suddenly I can’t remember the next step. And these are things I’ve done for years, so it’s not like I’m learning something new. It’s like my brain just stops giving me access to what I know.\n\nSo if someone was talking to me during that moment, I might not answer right away, and maybe to them I seem unaware. But I don’t know that I’m totally unaware of the room or the road or people around me. I’m more stuck on the fact that I can’t think through the thing I’m supposed to be doing. It’s scary and embarrassing, because I’m standing there with something familiar in front of me and I just… can’t get the steps to come back.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank spells, do you sometimes realize you can’t remember part of what happened during it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes, I think. It’s not like a whole chunk of time is missing exactly, at least not that I can always prove, but after it happens I’ll realize I don’t really know what I just did for a few seconds, or what someone said to me right then.\n\nThe bigger thing is that my mind just goes blank in the middle of something I should know. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps. And these aren’t new tasks—I’ve done them for years. So afterward I’m trying to piece it together, like, “Did I already check that part? Did someone ask me something? Was I supposed to move on to the next step?” It’s hard to explain because it’s not always a clear memory loss, like waking up and not knowing where I am. It’s more like there’s this gap where my thinking just stopped working, and then I’m left trying to catch up and figure out what I missed.\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me enough to come in. It’s happening at the worst times, when I’m supposed to be focused and I should absolutely know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found that time had passed or that you had done something during one of these spells that you couldn’t remember afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve found that I did some whole thing and then had no memory of it, like… I can’t point to a completed task and say, “I don’t remember doing that at all.” It’s more that I’ll be in the middle of something very familiar and my mind just goes blank.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember what comes next. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Not in a dramatic way, like I’m passed out or anything, but people have noticed that I’ll sort of freeze or stare for a few seconds and not answer like I normally would.\n\nIt usually happens when I’m right in the middle of something I should know how to do. That’s honestly why I came in—my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll be holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps. Steps I’ve done for years. And if someone says something to me right then, I guess from the outside it probably looks like I’m just staring and not responding.\n\nSometimes someone will be waiting on me, and I can tell afterward that there was this pause where I didn’t answer. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to ignore them. I’m just stuck for that moment, like my brain won’t move to the next part. Then I come back into it and try to cover it, but I know they’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Between these blank moments, do you spend most days feeling hard-to-control worry about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I don’t know that I’ve timed them exactly. They feel… not identical every time, but the same kind of thing, if that makes sense. It’s usually not like I’m gone for hours or anything I can measure clearly. It’s more like I’m in the middle of something and my mind just drops out.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I’m having these moments where my mind just goes blank, and it’s starting to scare me. Like, at work, I’ve done the same road-closure paperwork and the same stop-slow paddle routine for years, I know the steps, I really do—but lately I’ll be looking right at the forms or holding the paddle, and it’s like the instructions just disappear out of my head.\n\nIt happens at the worst times, too, not just when I’m sitting around. That’s why I’m here. I’m still there, like I can see the cones, the cars, the paperwork in front of me, or I’m holding the stop-slow paddle and I know I’m supposed to be doing the job. But the part that tells me what comes next just… drops out.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I’m aware enough to realize something is wrong, which almost makes it worse. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms I’ve filled out so many times, or standing there with the paddle, and suddenly I cannot pull up the steps I’ve done for years. It’s not like I don’t know where I am. It’s more like my thinking goes empty right when I need it, and then I’m stuck trying to piece together what I already did and what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank moments tend to happen when you’re feeling especially stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think stress makes it worse, or at least it’s when I notice it the most, because there’s pressure to get it right. But it’s not only like, “Oh, I’m nervous so I forgot something.” It feels bigger than that.\n\nLike I’ll be at work, and I’ll have the road-closure forms in front of me—forms I’ve done over and over, for years—and I’ll just stare at them and suddenly I can’t remember the order. Or I’m holding the stop-slow paddle, and I know this is my job, I know I know how to do it, but the steps just vanish. My mind goes blank right there, usually at the worst possible time, when I need to be quick and clear.\n\nAnd then of course I get anxious because I’m blanking, so it sort of feeds itself. I start thinking, “Did I already do that part? What comes next? Am I missing something important?” But the blankness feels like it comes first sometimes, and then the stress piles on after.\n\nDoctor: During these blank moments, do you ever find yourself doing repetitive movements you didn’t mean to, like lip-smacking, chewing, or fumbling with your hands?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve noticed, anyway. I mean, I’m usually so focused on the fact that my mind has gone blank that I’m trying really hard to look normal and not make a mistake, so I might miss little things. But I haven’t had anyone say, like, “Why are you chewing like that?” or “Why are you moving your hands funny?” or anything.\n\nI do sometimes fidget when I’m anxious, like holding the paperwork tighter or adjusting my grip on the paddle, but that feels more like me being tense and trying to get myself back on track. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by repetitive movements. I haven’t noticed lip-smacking or chewing or anything that feels automatic like that.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank moments passes, can you remember what happened during it?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… but it’s patchy in a weird way. I can usually remember that I was standing there, or sitting with the road-closure forms in front of me, and I remember staring at them or holding the stop-slow paddle and thinking, “Why can’t I do this?” So it’s not like the whole thing disappears afterward.\n\nWhat I don’t always remember clearly is what I was thinking right in the middle, because there almost isn’t anything there to remember. It’s like my mind just goes blank at the worst possible moment. I’ll be looking at something I’ve done for years, something routine, and suddenly I can’t bring up the next step. Then once it passes, I can remember being stuck, and I can remember the panic of trying to figure out, “Did I already do that? What comes next?” But the actual blank part is just… empty.\n\nSo I guess I remember the situation around it, but not in a useful way. I don’t come out of it with a clear memory of the steps or what I did during it. That’s part of why it scares me, because these are things I should know automatically by now.\n\nDoctor: About how long does a typical blank moment last?\n\nPatient: It’s hard to pin down exactly, because when it happens I’m usually trying so hard to get myself back, if that makes sense. It’s not like I’m calmly timing it. But most of the time it feels like maybe seconds to a minute or two… though honestly it can feel much longer in the moment, because it happens right when I need to know what I’m doing.\n\nLike with the road-closure forms, I’ll just be looking at them and suddenly the order is gone. I know I’ve done them for years, I know they should be familiar, but for that stretch I just can’t pull the steps into my head. Or with the stop-slow paddle, it might only be a short time before I can sort of force myself back into the routine, but standing there with traffic and people expecting me to know what comes next, it feels endless.\n\nSo… typical? I’d say usually brief, maybe under a couple minutes, but it’s long enough to scare me because it’s at the worst possible times.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank moments ends, do you feel confused?\n\nPatient: Yes… kind of. Not confused like I don’t know where I am or who I’m with, but confused about what I was supposed to be doing. Like I’ll come out of it and I’m still looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and I know I should know the next step because I’ve done it for years, but it’s like the order of it gets scrambled or just disappears for a minute.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst times. I’ll be in the middle of something routine, something I shouldn’t even have to think that hard about, and suddenly my mind just goes blank. Then afterward I’m left trying to piece it back together—like, “Okay, did I already do this part? What comes next? Am I missing something obvious?” And that makes me feel confused and embarrassed, because it’s work I know I know, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, after it ends I’m usually confused for a bit, mostly trying to get the steps back in the right order and act like nothing happened.\n\nDoctor: During one of these blank moments, if someone talks to you, can you respond right away?\n\nPatient: Not always right away, no. I mean, I can sometimes hear that someone has said something, like I register there’s a voice or that they’re waiting for me, but the answer doesn’t come immediately because my mind is still stuck on… nothing, almost.\n\nLike if I’m staring at the road-closure forms and someone asks, “Did you finish that part?” or “What’s next?” I might just look at them for a second too long because I suddenly can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s embarrassing, because I know I should be able to answer quickly. Same with the stop-slow paddle—if I’m holding it and someone talks to me, I might respond, but it can be delayed or kind of uncertain, because I’m trying to pull my thinking back together.\n\nSo I don’t think I’m completely unaware, exactly. It’s more like there’s a lag. I hear them, but my brain won’t give me the next step or the right words fast enough.\n\nDoctor: Right before one of these blank moments starts, do you ever notice a strange smell or taste?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed anything like a weird smell or taste right before it happens.\n\nUsually it’s more like I’m just doing the task and then suddenly I realize my mind has gone blank. I’m not aware of a warning sign like that. If there is one, I’m not catching it, because I’m usually focused on the forms or the road or what I’m supposed to be doing, and then it just kind of… drops out.\n\nSo, no, not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever told you that during one of these blank moments you just stare and don’t respond for a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m passed out or anything, but people have noticed that I’ll sort of freeze or stare for a few seconds and not answer like I normally would.\n\nIt usually happens when I’m right in the middle of something I should know how to do. That’s honestly why I came in—my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll be holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps. Steps I’ve done for years. And if someone says something to me right then, I guess from the outside it probably looks like I’m just staring and not responding.\n\nSometimes someone will be waiting on me, and I can tell afterward that there was this pause where I didn’t answer. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to ignore them. I’m just stuck for that moment, like my brain won’t move to the next part. Then I come back into it and try to cover it, but I know they’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Between these blank moments, do you spend most days feeling hard-to-control worry about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I do think about things, and I get concerned about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “most days” or “a lot of different things” in the way you mean.\n\nLike, when you say hard-to-control worry, I’m trying to figure out if you mean worry that just runs on its own and I can’t shut it off, or if you mean being stressed about what’s happening. Because I can get caught up thinking about something, especially if something went wrong or I’m afraid I missed something, but I don’t know that I’ve really been keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it as a clear pattern like that, at least not enough to say yes confidently.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that during one of these blank spells you seemed completely unaware of them or of what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely unaware. That’s the part I have trouble judging, because from inside it doesn’t feel like I’m gone somewhere exactly. It’s more like my thinking just blanks out.\n\nLike, that’s really why I came in. I’ll be right there at work, looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and then suddenly I can’t remember the next step. And these are things I’ve done for years, so it’s not like I’m learning something new. It’s like my brain just stops giving me access to what I know.\n\nSo if someone was talking to me during that moment, I might not answer right away, and maybe to them I seem unaware. But I don’t know that I’m totally unaware of the room or the road or people around me. I’m more stuck on the fact that I can’t think through the thing I’m supposed to be doing. It’s scary and embarrassing, because I’m standing there with something familiar in front of me and I just… can’t get the steps to come back.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank spells, do you sometimes realize you can’t remember part of what happened during it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes, I think. It’s not like a whole chunk of time is missing exactly, at least not that I can always prove, but after it happens I’ll realize I don’t really know what I just did for a few seconds, or what someone said to me right then.\n\nThe bigger thing is that my mind just goes blank in the middle of something I should know. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps. And these aren’t new tasks—I’ve done them for years. So afterward I’m trying to piece it together, like, “Did I already check that part? Did someone ask me something? Was I supposed to move on to the next step?” It’s hard to explain because it’s not always a clear memory loss, like waking up and not knowing where I am. It’s more like there’s this gap where my thinking just stopped working, and then I’m left trying to catch up and figure out what I missed.\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me enough to come in. It’s happening at the worst times, when I’m supposed to be focused and I should absolutely know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found that time had passed or that you had done something during one of these spells that you couldn’t remember afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve found that I did some whole thing and then had no memory of it, like… I can’t point to a completed task and say, “I don’t remember doing that at all.” It’s more that I’ll be in the middle of something very familiar and my mind just goes blank.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember what comes next. And that’s not something that should be happening, because I’ve done those steps for years. It’s not complicated to me normally. But in that moment it’s like the order of it disappears, and I’m standing there trying to pull it back.\n\nSo afterward I might feel like time slipped a little because I’m trying to figure out, “Wait, did I already do that part? Was I supposed to be on the next step?” But I’m not sure it’s the same as doing things and not remembering them. The part I know for sure is that my thinking just cuts out at the worst times, with things I should absolutely know how to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost control of your bladder during one of these blank spells?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t noticed anything like that happening. After these spells I’m usually embarrassed or scared because I’ve lost my place mentally, but I haven’t found that I wet myself or anything like that.\n\nI guess if it had happened at work I’d probably know, because I’m standing outside or around other people, and that would be pretty obvious. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank spells tend to last about the same amount of time each time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve timed them exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it sleepy, exactly. I don’t usually feel like I need to lie down right after, or like I’m going to nod off. It’s more like I feel kind of rattled and worn out mentally, if that makes sense.

Like, after it happens, I’ll be trying to replay what just happened in my head — “Did I miss something? Did someone notice? Am I supposed to be doing the next step already?” — and that takes a lot out of me. So I might feel drained, but I don’t know if that’s from the spell itself or from the panic afterward.

I haven’t really noticed a pattern where I get very tired or suddenly sleepy every time. It’s more embarrassment and stress, and then I’m tense for a while because I’m worried it might happen again.

It just goes blank at the worst possible time, and then I’m standing there trying to get the order back in my head.\n\nSo as far as the length… I’d say it feels brief, but it can feel longer because I’m panicking a little and trying to act normal while I’m figuring out what I’m supposed to do next. I don’t think I can say it’s the exact same amount of time each time. It’s more that the pattern is the same — my thinking just stops working for a moment when I really need it not to.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank spells, do you feel very tired or sleepy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sleepy, exactly. It’s more like I feel kind of rattled and worn out mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, after it happens, I’ll be trying to replay what just happened in my head — “Did I miss something? Did someone notice? Am I supposed to be doing the next step already?” — and that takes a lot out of me. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever lost control of your bladder during one of these blank spells?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t noticed anything like that happening. After these spells I’m usually embarrassed or scared because I’ve lost my place mentally, but I haven’t found that I wet myself or anything like that.\n\nI guess if it had happened at work I’d probably know, because I’m standing outside or around other people, and that would be pretty obvious. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank spells tend to last about the same amount of time each time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve timed them exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not anything that stands out to me. I mean, I’ve bumped my head here and there like anybody, I guess, but I don’t remember some accident or fall or anything where I thought, “Oh, this started after that.”

When you say head injury, do you mean like a concussion, or being knocked out? Because if that’s what you mean, no, I can’t point to anything like that before the blank spells started. I haven’t had some big head trauma that I know of. It’s more that the blanking out just became something I noticed happening, and I couldn’t really tie it to an injury or a specific incident.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I’m having these moments where my mind just goes blank, and it’s starting to scare me. Like, at work, I’ve done the same road-closure paperwork and the same stop-slow paddle routine for years, I know the steps, I really do—but lately I’ll be looking right at the forms or holding the paddle, and it’s like the instructions just disappear out of my head.\n\nIt happens at the worst times, too, not just when I’m sitting around. I’ll be standing there thinking, okay, I should know what comes next, and there’s just nothing. Blank. And then I get stuck trying to remember what I already did and what I’m supposed to do next. That’s why I’m here. It feels like my own mind isn’t reliable right when I need it to be.\n\nDoctor: When these blank moments happen, do you lose awareness of what’s going on around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I fully black out or anything, if that’s what you mean. I’m still there, like I can see the cones, the cars, the paperwork in front of me, or I’m holding the stop-slow paddle and I know I’m supposed to be doing the job. But the part that tells me what comes next just… drops out.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I’m aware enough to realize something is wrong, which almost makes it worse. I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms I’ve filled out so many times, or standing there with the paddle, and suddenly I cannot pull up the steps I’ve done for years. It’s not like I don’t know where I am. It’s more like my thinking goes empty right when I need it, and then I’m stuck trying to piece together what I already did and what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank moments tend to happen when you’re feeling especially stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think stress makes it worse, or at least it’s when I notice it the most, because there’s pressure to get it right. But it’s not only like, “Oh, I’m nervous so I forgot something.” It feels bigger than that.\n\nLike I’ll be at work, and I’ll have the road-closure forms in front of me—forms I’ve done over and over, for years—and I’ll just stare at them and suddenly I can’t remember the order. Or I’m holding the stop-slow paddle, and I know this is my job, I know I know how to do it, but the steps just vanish. I start thinking, “Did I already do that part? What comes next? I don’t know if that’s what you mean by repetitive movements. I haven’t noticed lip-smacking or chewing or anything that feels automatic like that.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank moments passes, can you remember what happened during it?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… but it’s patchy in a weird way. I can usually remember that I was standing there, or sitting with the road-closure forms in front of me, and I remember staring at them or holding the stop-slow paddle and thinking, “Why can’t I do this?” So it’s not like the whole thing disappears afterward.\n\nWhat I don’t always remember clearly is what I was thinking right in the middle, because there almost isn’t anything there to remember. I don’t come out of it with a clear memory of the steps or what I did during it. It’s not like I’m calmly timing it. But most of the time it feels like maybe seconds to a minute or two… though honestly it can feel much longer in the moment, because it happens right when I need to know what I’m doing.\n\nLike with the road-closure forms, I’ll just be looking at them and suddenly the order is gone. I know I’ve done them for years, I know they should be familiar, but for that stretch I just can’t pull the steps into my head. Or with the stop-slow paddle, it might only be a short time before I can sort of force myself back into the routine, but standing there with traffic and people expecting me to know what comes next, it feels endless.\n\nSo… typical? I’d say usually brief, maybe under a couple minutes, but it’s long enough to scare me because it’s at the worst possible times.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank moments ends, do you feel confused?\n\nPatient: Yes… kind of. Not confused like I don’t know where I am or who I’m with, but confused about what I was supposed to be doing. Like I’ll come out of it and I’m still looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and I know I should know the next step because I’ve done it for years, but it’s like the order of it gets scrambled or just disappears for a minute.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst times. I’ll be in the middle of something routine, something I shouldn’t even have to think that hard about, and suddenly my mind just goes blank. Then afterward I’m left trying to piece it back together—like, “Okay, did I already do this part? What comes next? Am I missing something obvious?” And that makes me feel confused and embarrassed, because it’s work I know I know, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, after it ends I’m usually confused for a bit, mostly trying to get the steps back in the right order and act like nothing happened.\n\nDoctor: During one of these blank moments, if someone talks to you, can you respond right away?\n\nPatient: Not always right away, no. I mean, I can sometimes hear that someone has said something, like I register there’s a voice or that they’re waiting for me, but the answer doesn’t come immediately because my mind is still stuck on… nothing, almost.\n\nLike if I’m staring at the road-closure forms and someone asks, “Did you finish that part?” or “What’s next?” I might just look at them for a second too long because I suddenly can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s more like there’s a lag. I haven’t noticed anything like a weird smell or taste right before it happens.\n\nUsually it’s more like I’m just doing the task and then suddenly I realize my mind has gone blank. That’s honestly why I came in—my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll be holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps. Steps I’ve done for years. Because I can get caught up thinking about something, especially if something went wrong or I’m afraid I missed something, but I don’t know that I’ve really been keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it as a clear pattern like that, at least not enough to say yes confidently.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that during one of these blank spells you seemed completely unaware of them or of what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely unaware. But I don’t know that I’m totally unaware of the room or the road or people around me. I’m more stuck on the fact that I can’t think through the thing I’m supposed to be doing. It’s scary and embarrassing, because I’m standing there with something familiar in front of me and I just… can’t get the steps to come back.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank spells, do you sometimes realize you can’t remember part of what happened during it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes, I think. It’s not like a whole chunk of time is missing exactly, at least not that I can always prove, but after it happens I’ll realize I don’t really know what I just did for a few seconds, or what someone said to me right then.\n\nThe bigger thing is that my mind just goes blank in the middle of something I should know. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps. And these aren’t new tasks—I’ve done them for years. So afterward I’m trying to piece it together, like, “Did I already check that part? Did someone ask me something? Was I supposed to move on to the next step?” It’s hard to explain because it’s not always a clear memory loss, like waking up and not knowing where I am. It’s more like there’s this gap where my thinking just stopped working, and then I’m left trying to catch up and figure out what I missed.\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me enough to come in. It’s happening at the worst times, when I’m supposed to be focused and I should absolutely know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found that time had passed or that you had done something during one of these spells that you couldn’t remember afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve found that I did some whole thing and then had no memory of it, like… I can’t point to a completed task and say, “I don’t remember doing that at all.” It’s more that I’ll be in the middle of something very familiar and my mind just goes blank.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember what comes next. I’d say usually brief, maybe under a couple minutes, but it’s long enough to scare me because it’s at the worst possible times.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank moments ends, do you feel confused?\n\nPatient: Yes… kind of. Not confused like I don’t know where I am or who I’m with, but confused about what I was supposed to be doing. Like I’ll come out of it and I’m still looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and I know I should know the next step because I’ve done it for years, but it’s like the order of it gets scrambled or just disappears for a minute.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because it keeps happening at the worst times. I’ll be in the middle of something routine, something I shouldn’t even have to think that hard about, and suddenly my mind just goes blank. Then afterward I’m left trying to piece it back together—like, “Okay, did I already do this part? What comes next? Am I missing something obvious?” And that makes me feel confused and embarrassed, because it’s work I know I know, if that makes sense.\n\nSo yes, after it ends I’m usually confused for a bit, mostly trying to get the steps back in the right order and act like nothing happened.\n\nDoctor: During one of these blank moments, if someone talks to you, can you respond right away?\n\nPatient: Not always right away, no. I mean, I can sometimes hear that someone has said something, like I register there’s a voice or that they’re waiting for me, but the answer doesn’t come immediately because my mind is still stuck on… nothing, almost.\n\nLike if I’m staring at the road-closure forms and someone asks, “Did you finish that part?” or “What’s next?” I might just look at them for a second too long because I suddenly can’t remember the steps I’ve done for years. It’s embarrassing, because I know I should be able to answer quickly. Same with the stop-slow paddle—if I’m holding it and someone talks to me, I might respond, but it can be delayed or kind of uncertain, because I’m trying to pull my thinking back together.\n\nSo I don’t think I’m completely unaware, exactly. It’s more like there’s a lag. I hear them, but my brain won’t give me the next step or the right words fast enough.\n\nDoctor: Right before one of these blank moments starts, do you ever notice a strange smell or taste?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I haven’t noticed anything like a weird smell or taste right before it happens.\n\nUsually it’s more like I’m just doing the task and then suddenly I realize my mind has gone blank. I’m not aware of a warning sign like that. If there is one, I’m not catching it, because I’m usually focused on the forms or the road or what I’m supposed to be doing, and then it just kind of… drops out.\n\nSo, no, not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever told you that during one of these blank moments you just stare and don’t respond for a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not in a dramatic way, like I’m passed out or anything, but people have noticed that I’ll sort of freeze or stare for a few seconds and not answer like I normally would.\n\nIt usually happens when I’m right in the middle of something I should know how to do. That’s honestly why I came in—my mind keeps going blank at the worst possible times. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll be holding the stop-slow paddle, and suddenly I just can’t remember the steps. Steps I’ve done for years. And if someone says something to me right then, I guess from the outside it probably looks like I’m just staring and not responding.\n\nSometimes someone will be waiting on me, and I can tell afterward that there was this pause where I didn’t answer. It’s embarrassing because I’m not trying to ignore them. I’m just stuck for that moment, like my brain won’t move to the next part. Then I come back into it and try to cover it, but I know they’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Between these blank moments, do you spend most days feeling hard-to-control worry about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean… I do think about things, and I get concerned about things, but I don’t know if I’d call it “most days” or “a lot of different things” in the way you mean.\n\nLike, when you say hard-to-control worry, I’m trying to figure out if you mean worry that just runs on its own and I can’t shut it off, or if you mean being stressed about what’s happening. Because I can get caught up thinking about something, especially if something went wrong or I’m afraid I missed something, but I don’t know that I’ve really been keeping track of it that way.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed it as a clear pattern like that, at least not enough to say yes confidently.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that during one of these blank spells you seemed completely unaware of them or of what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely unaware. That’s the part I have trouble judging, because from inside it doesn’t feel like I’m gone somewhere exactly. It’s more like my thinking just blanks out.\n\nLike, that’s really why I came in. I’ll be right there at work, looking at the road-closure forms or holding the stop-slow paddle, and then suddenly I can’t remember the next step. And these are things I’ve done for years, so it’s not like I’m learning something new. It’s like my brain just stops giving me access to what I know.\n\nSo if someone was talking to me during that moment, I might not answer right away, and maybe to them I seem unaware. But I don’t know that I’m totally unaware of the room or the road or people around me. I’m more stuck on the fact that I can’t think through the thing I’m supposed to be doing. It’s scary and embarrassing, because I’m standing there with something familiar in front of me and I just… can’t get the steps to come back.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank spells, do you sometimes realize you can’t remember part of what happened during it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes, I think. It’s not like a whole chunk of time is missing exactly, at least not that I can always prove, but after it happens I’ll realize I don’t really know what I just did for a few seconds, or what someone said to me right then.\n\nThe bigger thing is that my mind just goes blank in the middle of something I should know. Like I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps. And these aren’t new tasks—I’ve done them for years. So afterward I’m trying to piece it together, like, “Did I already check that part? Did someone ask me something? Was I supposed to move on to the next step?” It’s hard to explain because it’s not always a clear memory loss, like waking up and not knowing where I am. It’s more like there’s this gap where my thinking just stopped working, and then I’m left trying to catch up and figure out what I missed.\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me enough to come in. It’s happening at the worst times, when I’m supposed to be focused and I should absolutely know what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found that time had passed or that you had done something during one of these spells that you couldn’t remember afterward?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve found that I did some whole thing and then had no memory of it, like… I can’t point to a completed task and say, “I don’t remember doing that at all.” It’s more that I’ll be in the middle of something very familiar and my mind just goes blank.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’ll be looking at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember what comes next. And that’s not something that should be happening, because I’ve done those steps for years. It’s not complicated to me normally. But in that moment it’s like the order of it disappears, and I’m standing there trying to pull it back.\n\nSo afterward I might feel like time slipped a little because I’m trying to figure out, “Wait, did I already do that part? Was I supposed to be on the next step?” But I’m not sure it’s the same as doing things and not remembering them. The part I know for sure is that my thinking just cuts out at the worst times, with things I should absolutely know how to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost control of your bladder during one of these blank spells?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t noticed anything like that happening. After these spells I’m usually embarrassed or scared because I’ve lost my place mentally, but I haven’t found that I wet myself or anything like that.\n\nI guess if it had happened at work I’d probably know, because I’m standing outside or around other people, and that would be pretty obvious. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do these blank spells tend to last about the same amount of time each time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve timed them exactly. They feel… not identical every time, but the same kind of thing, if that makes sense. It’s usually not like I’m gone for hours or anything I can measure clearly. It’s more like I’m in the middle of something and my mind just drops out.\n\nLike I’ll be looking right at the road-closure forms, or I’ll have the stop-slow paddle in my hand, and suddenly I can’t remember the steps. And that’s what bothers me, because these are steps I’ve done for years. It’s not like I’m new at it or confused because the job changed. It just goes blank at the worst possible time, and then I’m standing there trying to get the order back in my head.\n\nSo as far as the length… I’d say it feels brief, but it can feel longer because I’m panicking a little and trying to act normal while I’m figuring out what I’m supposed to do next. I don’t think I can say it’s the exact same amount of time each time. It’s more that the pattern is the same — my thinking just stops working for a moment when I really need it not to.\n\nDoctor: After one of these blank spells, do you feel very tired or sleepy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sleepy, exactly. I don’t usually feel like I need to lie down right after, or like I’m going to nod off. It’s more like I feel kind of rattled and worn out mentally, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, after it happens, I’ll be trying to replay what just happened in my head — “Did I miss something? Did someone notice? Am I supposed to be doing the next step already?” — and that takes a lot out of me. It’s more embarrassment and stress, and then I’m tense for a while because I’m worried it might happen again.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any head injury before these blank spells started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything that stands out to me. Not completely, no. I mean… sometimes I can get back to the task and look like I’m functioning, but I don’t feel normal-normal, if that makes sense.

The whole reason I came in is because my mind keeps doing this blank thing at the worst times. Like I’ll be standing there with the road-closure forms, or holding the stop-slow paddle, and these are things I’ve done for years, so I should just know the next step without even thinking. So between them, I guess I’m better than during the blank spell, but I’m not completely normal because I’m kind of waiting for my mind to drop out again. It makes me feel like I can’t trust my own head the way I used to.
